### Foreign relations

Main article: [Foreign relations of Cuba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_relations_of_Cuba)

See also: [Cuban medical internationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_medical_internationalism)



[Fidel Castro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fidel_Castro) and [Ali Khamenei](385653.md) at a meeting of the [Non-Aligned Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-Aligned_Movement) in Zimbabwe, 3 September 1986

Cuba is a founding member of the [United Nations](31769.md), [G77](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Group_of_77), [Non-Aligned Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-Aligned_Movement), [Organisation of African, Caribbean and Pacific States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organisation_of_African,_Caribbean_and_Pacific_States), [ALBA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ALBA), and [Organization of American States](22788.md). Cuba has conducted a foreign policy that is uncharacteristic of such a minor, developing country.[^cite_note-Domínguez_1989_6-232][^cite_note-Feinsilver_1989_2-233] Under Castro, Cuba was heavily involved in wars in Africa, Central America and Asia. Cuba supported [Algeria](358.md) in 1961–1965[^cite_note-Gleijeses_1996_159,161-234] and sent tens of thousands of troops to [Angola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_intervention_in_Angola) during the [Angolan Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angolan_Civil_War).[^cite_note-Gleijeses_2010_327-235] Other countries that featured Cuban involvement include [Ethiopia](187749.md),[^cite_note-Gleijeses_2002_392-236][^cite_note-237] [Guinea](12176.md),[^cite_note-Gleijeses_1997_50-238] [Guinea-Bissau](12186.md),[^cite_note-Gleijeses_1997_45-239] [Mozambique](19301.md),[^cite_note-Gleijeses_2002_227-240] and [Yemen](350939.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERamazani1975\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid7ETnbmUmxoUCpgPA91_91\]-241] Lesser known actions include the 1959 missions to the [Dominican Republic](8060.md).[^cite_note-242] The expedition failed, but a prominent monument to its members was erected in their memory in Santo Domingo by the Dominican government, and they feature prominently at the country's Memorial Museum of the Resistance.[^cite_note-243]

In 2008, the European Union and Cuba agreed to resume full relations and cooperation activities.[^cite_note-244] Cuba is a founding member of the [Bolivarian Alliance for the Americas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bolivarian_Alliance_for_the_Americas).[^cite_note-245] Tens of thousands of Cuban medical personnel have worked abroad,[^cite_note-246] with as many as 30,000 doctors in Venezuela alone via the two countries' oil-for-doctors programme.[^cite_note-247]



[Raúl Castro](221749.md) with Russian President [Vladimir Putin](32817.md) in New York City, 28 September 2015



Cuban leader [Miguel Díaz-Canel](38630938.md) with Brazilian President [Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva](99328.md) in Paris, France, 22 June 2023

In the wake of the [Russian invasion of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine) and the ongoing international isolation of Russia, Cuba emerged as one of the few countries that maintained friendly relations with the Russian Federation.[^cite_note-248][^cite_note-249] Diaz-Canel visited [Vladimir Putin](32817.md) in Moscow in November 2022, where the two leaders opened a monument of Fidel Castro, as well as speaking out against U.S. sanctions against Russia and Cuba.[^cite_note-250]

#### Embargo by the United States (1960–present)

Main article: [United States embargo against Cuba](1105954.md)

&#x20;Since 1960, the U.S. embargo on Cuba stands as one of the longest-running trade and economic measures in bilateral relations history, having endured for over six decades. This action was initiated in response to a wave of nationalizations that impacted American properties valued at over US\$1 billion. U.S.[^cite_note-\:6-40] American diplomat [Lester D. Mallory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lester_D._Mallory) wrote an internal memo on 6 April 1960, arguing in favor of an embargo: "The only foreseeable means of alienating internal support is through disenchantment and disaffection based on economic dissatisfaction and hardship. \[...] to decrease monetary and real wages, to bring about hunger, desperation and overthrow of government."[^cite_note-251][^cite_note-252] President Dwight Eisenhower instated an embargo that prohibited all exports to Cuba, with the exception of medicines and certain foods.[^cite_note-\:6-40] This measure was intensified in 1962 under the administration of John F. Kennedy, extending the restrictions to Cuban imports, based on the Foreign Assistance Act approved by Congress in 1961.[^cite_note-\:6-40] During the Missile Crisis in 1962, the United States even imposed a naval blockade on Cuba, but this was lifted following the resolution of the crisis. The embargo, however, remained in place and has been modified on several occasions over the years.[^cite_note-\:6-40]



Propaganda sign in front of the [United States Interests Section in Havana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Interests_Section_in_Havana)

The [Cuban Democracy Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_Democracy_Act) of 1992 states that sanctions will continue "so long as it continues to refuse to move toward democratization and greater respect for human rights".[^cite_note-253] The [UN General Assembly](31957.md) has passed a resolution every year since 1992 condemning the embargo and stating that it violates the [Charter of the United Nations](88805.md) and international law.[^cite_note-254] Cuba considers the embargo a human rights violation.[^cite_note-255] The impact and effectiveness of the embargo have been subjects of intense debate. While some argue it has been "extraordinarily porous" and is not the primary cause of Cuba's economic hardships, others see it as a pressure mechanism aimed at driving change in the Cuban government.[^cite_note-\:6-40] According to Arturo Lopez Levy, a professor of international relations, it would be more appropriate to refer to the measure as a "[blockade](293054.md)" or "[siege](26905.md)", as it goes beyond mere trade restrictions.[^cite_note-\:6-40] Other critics of the Cuban government argue that the embargo has been used by the government as an excuse to justify its own economic and political shortcomings.[^cite_note-\:6-40]

In 1996, the United States passed the Cuban Liberty and Democratic Solidarity Act, better known as the [Helms–Burton Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helms%E2%80%93Burton_Act), which strengthens and continues the United States embargo against Cuba.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERoy2000\[\[Category\:Wikipedia_articles_needing_page_number_citations_from_December_2023\]\]<sup_class="noprint_Inline-Template_"_style="white-space\:nowrap;">\[<i>\[\[Wikipedia\:Citing_sources|<span_title="This_citation_requires_a_reference_to_the_specific_page_or_range_of_pages_in_which_the_material_appears. (December_2023)">page needed</span>\]\]</i>\]</sup>-256][^cite_note-258] In 2009, United States president [Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama) stated "the United States seeks a new beginning with Cuba",[^cite_note-259] and he reversed the [Bush Administration](3414021.md)'s prohibition on travel and remittances by Cuban-Americans from the United States to Cuba.[^cite_note-260] In 2014 an agreement between the United States and Cuba, popularly called the "[Cuban thaw](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_thaw)", brokered in part by Canada and [Pope Francis](1687680.md), began the process of restoring international relations between the two countries. They agreed to release political prisoners, and in 2015 the United States opened an embassy in Havana.[^cite_note-261][^cite_note-262][^cite_note-263][^cite_note-264][^cite_note-265][^cite_note-266] and reestablished diplomatic relations.[^cite_note-267] In 2015 Obama announced he would remove Cuba from the American government's list of nations that sponsor terrorism,[^cite_note-268][^cite_note-269] which Cuba reportedly welcomed as "fair".[^cite_note-270] On 17 September 2017, the United States considered closing its Cuban embassy following mysterious [medical symptoms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Havana_syndrome) experienced by its staff.[^cite_note-271]

The diplomatic relations set in place by Obama were later reversed by the [Trump](4848272.md) administration, which enacted new rules and re-enforced the business and travel restrictions which were loosened by the Obama administration.[^cite_note-272] These sanctions were inherited and strengthened by the [Biden](145422.md) administration.[^cite_note-273]

### Military

Main article: [Cuban Revolutionary Armed Forces](73251.md)

As of 2018, Cuba spent about US\$91.8 million on its armed forces or 2.9% of its GDP.[^cite_note-274] In 1985, Cuba devoted more than 10% of its GDP to military expenditures.[^cite_note-Hoyt_Atlantic-275] During the Cold War, Cuba built up the largest per capita armed forces in Latin America, funded and equipped by the Soviet Union.[^cite_note-military-276] From 1975 until the late 1980s, [Soviet military](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Armed_Forces) assistance enabled Cuba to upgrade its military capabilities. After the loss of Soviet subsidies, Cuba scaled down the numbers of military personnel, from 235,000 in 1994 to about 49,000 in 2021.[^cite_note-277]

In 2017, Cuba signed the UN treaty on the [Prohibition of Nuclear Weapons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_on_the_Prohibition_of_Nuclear_Weapons).[^cite_note-278] Since signing a defense pact with Belarus in January 2024, Cuba has upgraded its [S-125 Pechora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S-125_Pechora) air defense systems with Belarusian support,[^cite_note-279] but it does not have a modern networked air defense system.

According to the [Global Peace Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_Peace_Index), Cuba has a medium state of peace ranked 102 out of 163 countries.[^cite_note-280]

Cuba is considered a [military dictatorship](146728.md) in the [Democracy-Dictatorship Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democracy-Dictatorship_Index) and has been described as "a militarized society"[^cite_note-281] with the armed forces having long been the most powerful institution in the country.[^cite_note-282]

### Law enforcement

Main article: [Law enforcement in Cuba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Law_enforcement_in_Cuba)

See also: [Crime in Cuba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crime_in_Cuba)



A [Lada Riva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lada_Riva) police car in [Holguín](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holgu%C3%ADn)&#x20;

All law enforcement agencies are maintained under Cuba's Ministry of the Interior, which is supervised by the [Revolutionary Armed Forces](73251.md). The [Intelligence Directorate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Direcci%C3%B3n_de_Inteligencia) conducts intelligence operations and maintains close ties with the [Russian Federal Security Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Security_Service_\(Russia\)).[^cite_note-283] The US Justice Department considers Cuba a significant counterintelligence threat.[^cite_note-284]

### Censorship

Main article: [Censorship in Cuba](8110999.md)

The unconditional release of all [political prisoners](37307.md) was called for by the EU.[^cite_note-Laursen-285][^cite_note-286] In July 2010, the unofficial Cuban Human Rights Commission said there were 167 political prisoners in Cuba, a fall from 201 at the start of the year. The head of the commission stated that long prison sentences were being replaced by harassment and intimidation.[^cite_note-287] Cuba was ranked 19th by the number of imprisoned journalists of any nation in 2021 according to various sources, including the [Committee to Protect Journalists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Committee_to_Protect_Journalists) and Human Rights Watch.[^cite_note-288][^cite_note-289] Cuba ranks 171st out of 180 on the 2020 [World Press Freedom Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Press_Freedom_Index).[^cite_note-290]

The [Committees for the Defense of the Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Committees_for_the_Defense_of_the_Revolution) surveil their neighborhoods for "counter-revolutionary" activity.[^cite_note-291] Membership is not selective, but leading members are approved by the Cuban Communist Party.[^cite_note-292]

### Identity politics

Main articles: [LGBT rights in Cuba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBT_rights_in_Cuba) and [Women in Cuba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women_in_Cuba)



[Ladies in White](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ladies_in_White) demonstration in [Havana](49719.md) (April 2012)

Cuba performs in the top 25% of nations in [gender equality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gender_equality).[^cite_note-idea-225] In 2022 Cuba approved a referendum which [amended the Family Code](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Cuban_Family_Code_referendum) to legalise [same-sex marriage](92656.md) and allow [surrogate pregnancy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surrogate_pregnancy) and [same-sex adoption](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Same-sex_adoption). [Gender reassignment surgery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gender_reassignment_surgery) and [transgender hormone therapy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transgender_hormone_therapy) are provided free of charge under the national healthcare system. The government supported the proposed changes, but conservatives and parts of the opposition opposed them. [Official policies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBT_rights_in_Cuba) of the government from 1959 until the 1990s were hostile towards homosexuality, with the LGBT community marginalized on the basis of [heteronormativity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heteronormativity), traditional [gender roles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gender_role), and strict criteria for moralism.[^cite_note-\:02-183][^cite_note-293]

## Economy

Main article: [Economy of Cuba](5588.md)

Further information: [Dual economy of Cuba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual_economy_of_Cuba), [Rationing in Cuba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rationing_in_Cuba), [Sociolismo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sociolismo), and [United States embargo against Cuba](1105954.md)



Historical GDP per capita development

The Cuban state asserts its adherence to socialist principles in organizing its largely state-controlled [planned economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planned_economy). Most of the [means of production](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Means_of_production) are owned and run by the government, and most of the labor force is employed by the state. Recent years have seen a trend toward more private sector employment. By 2006, public sector employment was 78% and private sector 22%, compared to 91.8% to 8.2% in 1981.[^cite_note-294] Government spending is 78.1% of GDP.[^cite_note-295] Since the early 2010s, following the initial market reforms, it has become popular to describe the economy as being, or moving toward, [market socialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Market_socialism).[^cite_note-296][^cite_note-297][^cite_note-298] Any firm that hires a Cuban must pay the Cuban government, which in turn pays the employee in [Cuban pesos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_peso) (CUP).[^cite_note-299] After a reform in 2021, the minimum monthly wage is about 2100 CUP (US\$81), and the median monthly wage is about 4000 CUP (US\$155).[^cite_note-300] According to Carmelo Mesa-Lago, there is widespread consensus that the policies of the revolutionary government decreased poverty until the end of the Cold War.[^cite_note-301] Every Cuban household has a ration book (known as [libreta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Libreta)) entitling it to a monthly supply of food and other staples, which are provided at nominal cost.[^cite_note-dealsoff-302] Cuba’s economy is heavily impacted by the US trade embargo, which began in the 1960s.[^cite_note-idea-225]

According to the Havana Consulting Group, in 2014, [remittances](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Remittance) to Cuba were more than US\$3 million, the seventh highest in Latin America.[^cite_note-303] In 2019, remittances had grown to US\$6,616 million, but dropped down to US\$1,967 million in 2020, due to the COVID-19 pandemic.[^cite_note-304] The pandemic devastated Cuba's tourist industry, which along with a tightening of U.S. sanctions, led to large increase in emigration among younger working-age Cubans. It has been described as a crisis that is "threatening the stability" of Cuba, which "already has one of the hemisphere's oldest populations".[^cite_note-Augustin-11-12-22-305] According to a controversial 2023 report by the [Cuban Observatory of Human Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_Observatory_of_Human_Rights), 88% of Cuban citizens live in extreme poverty. The report states that Cubans were concerned about food security and the difficulty in acquiring basic goods.[^cite_note-\:3-306]

According to the [World Bank](36752.md), Cuba's [GDP per capita](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_\(PPP\)_per_capita) was \$9,500 as of 2020.[^cite_note-\:world_bank-307] But according to the [CIA World Factbook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_World_Factbook), it was \$12,300 as of 2016.[^cite_note-\:cia-308] The [United Nations Development Programme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Development_Programme) gave Cuba a [Human Development Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_Human_Development_Index) of 0.764 in 2021.[^cite_note-\:UNDP-309] The same United Nations agency estimated the country's [Multidimensional Poverty Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multidimensional_Poverty_Index) of 0.003 in 2023.[^cite_note-\:MPI-310]

In 2005, Cuba had exports of US\$2.4 billion, ranking 114 of 226 world countries, and imports of US\$6.9 billion, ranking 87 of 226 countries.[^cite_note-311] Its major export partners are Canada 17.7%, China 16.9%, Venezuela 12.5%, Netherlands 9%, and Spain 5.9% (2012).[^cite_note-factbook-312] Major exports include sugar, nickel, tobacco, fish, medical products, citrus fruits, and coffee;[^cite_note-factbook-312] imports include food, fuel, clothing, and machinery. Cuba presently holds debt in an amount estimated at \$13 billion,[^cite_note-313] approximately 38% of GDP.[^cite_note-314] Cuba's prior 35% supply of the world's export market for sugar has declined to 10% due to a variety of factors, including a global sugar commodity price drop that made Cuba less competitive on world markets.[^cite_note-315] It was announced in 2008 that wage caps would be abandoned to improve the nation's productivity.[^cite_note-316]

Cuba's leadership has called for reforms in the country's [agricultural system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agriculture_in_Cuba). In 2008, Raúl Castro began enacting agrarian reforms to boost food production, as at that time 80% of food was imported. The reforms aim to expand land use and increase efficiency.[^cite_note-food-317] Venezuela supplies Cuba with an estimated 110,000 barrels (17,000 m<sup>3</sup>) of oil per day in exchange for money and the services of some 44,000 Cubans, most of them medical personnel, in Venezuela.[^cite_note-VenezuelaReuters-318][^cite_note-VenezuelaAES-319]



Cubans are now permitted to own small businesses in certain sectors.

In 2010, Cubans were allowed to build their own houses. According to Raúl Castro, they could now improve their houses, but the government would not endorse these new houses or improvements.[^cite_note-320] There is virtually no homelessness in Cuba,[^cite_note-321][^cite_note-322] and 85% of Cubans own their homes[^cite_note-323] and pay no property taxes or mortgage interest.&#x20;

On 2 August 2011, *The New York Times* reported that Cuba reaffirmed its intent to legalize "buying and selling" of private property before the year's end. According to experts, the private sale of property could "transform Cuba more than any of the economic reforms announced by Cuban leader Raúl Castro's government".[^cite_note-324] It would cut more than one million state jobs, including party bureaucrats who resist the changes.[^cite_note-325] The reforms created what some call "New Cuban Economy".[^cite_note-326][^cite_note-327] In October 2013, Raúl said he intended to merge the two currencies, which eventually occurred in 2021.



Tobacco fields in [Viñales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vi%C3%B1ales_Valley)

In 2016, the *Miami Herald* wrote, "... about 27 percent of Cubans earn under \$50 per month; 34 percent earn the equivalent of \$50 to \$100 per month; and 20 percent earn \$101 to \$200. Twelve percent reported earning \$201 to \$500 a month; and almost 4 percent said their monthly earnings topped \$500, including 1.5 percent who said they earned more than \$1,000."[^cite_note-328]

In May 2019, Cuba imposed rationing of staples such as chicken, eggs, rice, beans, soap and other basic goods. (Some two-thirds of food in the country is imported.) A spokesperson blamed the increased U.S. trade embargo although economists believe that an equally important problem is the massive decline of aid from Venezuela and the failure of Cuba's state-run oil company which had subsidized fuel costs.[^cite_note-329]

In June 2019, the government announced an increase in public sector wages of about 300%, specifically for teachers and health personnel.[^cite_note-330] In October, the government allowed stores to purchase house equipment and similar items, using international currency, and send it to Cuba by emigration. The leaders of the government recognized that the new measures were unpopular but necessary to contain the capital flight to other countries as Panamá where Cuban citizens traveled and imported items to resell on the island. Other measures included allowing private companies to export and import, through state companies, resources to produce products and services in Cuba.



Hotel [Parque Central](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parque_Central,_Havana) in Havana

On 1 January 2021, Cuba's dual currency system was formally ended, and the [convertible Cuban peso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_convertible_peso) (CUC) was phased out, leaving the [Cuban peso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_peso) (CUP) as the country's sole currency unit. Cuban citizens had until June 2021 to exchange their CUCs. However, this devalued the Cuban peso and caused economic problems for people who had been previously paid in CUCs, particularly workers in the tourism industry.[^cite_note-331][^cite_note-aj112021-332][^cite_note-333] Also, in February, the government dictated new measures to the private sector, with prohibitions for only 124 activities,[^cite_note-334] in areas like national security, health and educational services.[^cite_note-335] The wages were increased again, between 4 and 9 times, for all the sectors. Also, new facilities were allowed to the state companies, with much more autonomy.[^cite_note-aj112021-332]

The first problem with the new reform, in terms of public opinion, were electricity prices, but that was amended quickly. Other measures corrected were in the prices for private farmers. In July 2020, Cuba opened new stores accepting only foreign currency while simultaneously eliminating a special tax on the U.S. dollar[^cite_note-336] to combat an economic crisis arising initially due to economic sanctions imposed by the Trump administration,[^cite_note-337] then later worsened by a lack of tourism during the [coronavirus pandemic](62750956.md). Cooperatives will play a larger role in the emerging Cuban economy. The transfer of state-run businesses into cooperatives could result in 20 – 30 percent of Cuba's workers being actively involved in cooperatives. All state-run restaurants (over 8,000) will be converted to worker owned cooperatives, and by 2017 the Cuban government expects there to be approximately 10,000 cooperatives operating.[^cite_note-338]

According to 2019 data, China stands as Cuba's main trading partner, followed by countries such as Spain, the Netherlands, Germany, and Cyprus. Cuba's main exports include tobacco, sugar, and alcoholic beverages, while it primarily imports chicken meat, wheat, corn, and condensed milk.[^cite_note-\:6-40]

### Resources

Cuba's natural resources include sugar, tobacco, fish, citrus fruits, [coffee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coffee_production_in_Cuba), beans, rice, potatoes, and livestock. Cuba's most important mineral resource is nickel, with 21% of total exports in 2011.[^cite_note-ITC-339] The output of Cuba's nickel mines that year was 71,000 tons, approaching 4% of world production.[^cite_note-USGS-340] As of 2013 its reserves were estimated at 5.5 million tons, over 7% of the world total.[^cite_note-USGS-340] [Sherritt International](4516089.md) of Canada operates a large nickel mining facility in [Moa](4428432.md). Cuba is also a major producer of refined [cobalt](24580536.md), a by-product of nickel mining.[^cite_note-torres-341]

Oil exploration in 2005 by the [US Geological Survey](23814944.md) revealed that the North Cuba Basin could produce about 4.6 billion barrels (730,000,000 m<sup>3</sup>) to 9.3 billion barrels (1.48×10<sup>9</sup> m<sup>3</sup>) of oil. In 2006, Cuba started to test-drill these locations for possible exploitation.[^cite_note-342]

### Tourism

Main article: [Tourism in Cuba](4736325.md)



Historic Centre of [Camagüey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camag%C3%BCey), a colonial city UNESCO World Heritage Site



[Varadero](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Varadero) resort area

Tourism was initially restricted to enclave resorts where tourists would be segregated from Cuban society, referred to as "enclave tourism" and "tourism apartheid".[^cite_note-343] Contact between foreign visitors and ordinary Cubans were *de facto* illegal between 1992 and 1997.[^cite_note-Corbett_2002_33-344] The rapid growth of tourism during the Special Period had widespread social and economic repercussions in Cuba, and led to speculation about the emergence of a two-tier economy.[^cite_note-345]

1.9 million tourists visited Cuba in 2003, predominantly from Canada and the European Union, generating revenue of US\$2.1 billion.[^cite_note-346] Cuba recorded 2,688,000 international tourists in 2011, the third-highest figure in the Caribbean (behind the Dominican Republic and Puerto Rico).[^cite_note-unwto-347] American tourism was incredibly limited due to the Cuban Missile Crisis until 2016, when most restrictions were limited but some remained in place.[^cite_note-348]

A study in 2018 indicated that Cuba has a potential for [mountaineering](20341.md) activity, and that mountaineering could be a key contributor to tourism, along with other activities, e.g. biking, diving, caving. Promoting these resources could contribute to regional development, prosperity, and well-being.[^cite_note-349]

The Cuban Justice minister downplays allegations of widespread [sex tourism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sex_tourism).[^cite_note-Justice_Minister-350] According to a Government of Canada travel advice website, "Cuba is actively working to prevent child sex tourism, and a number of tourists, including Canadians, have been convicted of offenses related to the corruption of minors aged 16 and under. Prison sentences range from 7 to 25 years."[^cite_note-351]

### Transport

Main article: [Transport in Cuba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transport_in_Cuba)

## Demographics

Main articles: [Cubans](1288758.md) and [Demographics of Cuba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_Cuba)

According to the official census of 2010, Cuba's population was 11,241,161, comprising 5,628,996 men and 5,612,165 women.[^cite_note-cubacensus2010-352] Its [birth rate](515898.md) (9.88 births per thousand population in 2006)[^cite_note-353] is one of the lowest in the [Western Hemisphere](73162.md). Although the country's population has grown by about four million people since 1961, the rate of growth slowed during that period, and the population began to decline in 2006, due to the country's low [fertility rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fertility_rate) (1.43 children per woman) coupled with emigration.[^cite_note-354]

### Largest cities

### Ethnoracial groups

[Ethnography of Cuba](1288758.md#Ethnography_of_Cuba)

Racial groups in Cuba (2012 census)

[^cite_note-356]

1. [Whites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Cubans) (64.1%)

2. [Mulattos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mulattos) (26.6%)

3. [Blacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Cubans) (9.26%)

Cuba's population descends primarily from three groups: [Spanish settlers and immigrants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_people)—drawn largely from [Andalusia](2736.md), [Galicia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galicia_\(Spain\)), [Asturias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asturias), and the [Canary Islands](5717.md)[^cite_note-357]—[sub-Saharan Africans](27067.md) transported to the island through the [transatlantic slave trade](49005.md),[^cite_note-358] and the pre-Columbian indigenous peoples, chiefly the [Taíno](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ta%C3%ADno) and [Ciboney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ciboney).[^cite_note-mendizabal2008-359][^cite_note-360]

Afro-Cubans are descended primarily from the [Yoruba people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yoruba_people), Bantu people from the [Congo basin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congo_basin), [Kalabari tribe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kalabari_tribe) and Arará from the [Dahomey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dahomey), as well as several thousand North African refugees, most notably the [Sahrawi Arabs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sahrawi_people) of [Western Sahara](33220.md).[^cite_note-361]

While [Afro-Cubans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afro-Cubans) and [Cubans of Spanish descent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Cubans) have endured as distinct ethnocultural units, no equivalent indigenous-descended group survived the catastrophic demographic collapse of the sixteenth century;[^cite_note-362] [Taíno](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ta%C3%ADno) ancestry nonetheless persists in the Cuban population at the genetic level, as evidenced by mitochondrial DNA studies.[^cite_note-mendizabal2008-359][^cite_note-363][^cite_note-364]

Cuba's population is therefore multiethnic, and intermarriage between these groups has been widespread across many centuries, producing a significant population of mixed ancestry—not as a product of recent admixture but of long-accumulated intergenerational intermarriage.[^cite_note-365][^cite_note-366] Consequently, there is significant discrepancy between different estimates of the country's racial composition. A 2014 study based on [ancestry-informative markers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancestry-informative_marker) found that [autosomal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autosome) genetic ancestry in Cuba is 72% European, 20% African, and 8% Indigenous.[^cite_note-plosgenetics.org-367]

The 2002 Cuban census, in which respondents self-identified, found that 65.05% of the population was white, while the 2012 census recorded approximately 27% of Cubans as mestizo o mulato and just under 10% as negro (Black).[^cite_note-368] The [2012 Cuban census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_Cuba_census) recorded, on the same basis, that 64.1% of the population was white (blanco), 26.6% mestizo o mulato (mixed race), and 9.3% Black (negro).[^cite_note-369] As with the 2002 census, these figures reflect self-perception rather than genetic ancestry or externally assigned classification, and have been widely contested by independent researchers who argue that they significantly under-report the Black and mixed-race population.[^cite_note-370]

These figures also diverge markedly from estimates produced by applying the [one-drop rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/One-drop_rule), the [hypodescent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hypodescent) convention historically specific to the United States, under which any traceable African ancestry classifies a person as Black. The [Institute for Cuban and Cuban-American Studies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Institute_for_Cuban_and_Cuban-American_Studies?action=edit\&redlink=1) at the [University of Miami](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Miami), using that framework, concluded that 62% of Cubans are Black.[^cite_note-371] However, the one-drop rule does not reflect Cuban or broader Latin American racial classification. In Cuba, as across most of Latin America, people of mixed African and European ancestry—commonly designated mulato—have historically constituted and understood themselves as a distinct category, neither defined in opposition to Blackness nor in denial of it, but in acknowledgement of a mixed heritage that carries its own social and cultural meaning. The Cuban census formally recognises this through its separate mestizo o mulato classification.[^cite_note-372][^cite_note-373] Applying a binary Black/white framework derived from U.S. legal and social history to Cuban racial self-identification therefore imposes a classificatory logic that Cuban society does not use and that systematically erases the identities of a substantial portion of the population.[^cite_note-374][^cite_note-375] In light of these methodological divergences, the [Minority Rights Group International](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minority_Rights_Group_International) has cautioned that "an objective assessment of the situation of [Afro-Cubans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afro-Cubans) remains problematic due to scant records and a paucity of systematic studies both pre- and post-revolution," and that estimates of the percentage of people of African descent in the Cuban population vary enormously, ranging from 33.9% to 62%.[^cite_note-376]

[Asians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asian_people) make up about 1% of the population and are largely of [Chinese ancestry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Cubans), followed by [Japanese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japanese_Cubans) and [Filipino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filipino_Cubans).[^cite_note-377][^cite_note-378] Many are descendants of farm laborers brought to the island by Spanish and American contractors during the 19th and early 20th century.[^cite_note-379] The current recorded number of Cubans with Chinese ancestry is 114,240.[^cite_note-380]

### Migration

#### Immigration

Main articles: [French immigration to Cuba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_immigration_to_Cuba) and [Spanish immigration to Cuba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_immigration_to_Cuba)

Immigration and emigration have played a prominent part in Cuba's demographic profile. Between the 18th and early 20th century, large waves of [Canarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canarian), [Catalan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catalans), [Andalusian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andalusians), [Galician](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galician_people), and other Spanish people immigrated to Cuba. Between 1899 and 1930 alone, nearly one million Spaniards entered Cuba, although many eventually returned to Spain.[^cite_note-381] Other prominent immigrant groups included French,[^cite_note-382] [Portuguese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_people), Italian, Russian, [Dutch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_people), [Greek](42056.md), British, and Irish, as well as small number of descendants of U.S. citizens who arrived in Cuba in the late 19th and early 20th centuries. As of 2015, the foreign-born population in Cuba was 13,336 inhabitants per the [World Bank](36752.md) data.[^cite_note-383]

#### Emigration

Main articles: [Cuban exile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_exile) and [Cuban immigration to the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_immigration_to_the_United_States)



[North Hudson, New Jersey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Havana_on_the_Hudson), is home to a large [Cuban American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_American) population.

Post-revolution Cuba has been characterized by significant levels of emigration, which has led to a [large and influential diaspora community](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_exile). During the three decades after January 1959, more than one million Cubans of all social classes—constituting 10% of the total population—[emigrated to the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_migration_to_Miami), a proportion that matches the extent of emigration to the U.S. from the Caribbean as a whole during that period.[^cite_note-384][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPedraza2007\[\[Category\:Wikipedia_articles_needing_page_number_citations_from_December_2023\]\]<sup_class="noprint_Inline-Template_"_style="white-space\:nowrap;">\[<i>\[\[Wikipedia\:Citing_sources|<span_title="This_citation_requires_a_reference_to_the_specific_page_or_range_of_pages_in_which_the_material_appears. (December_2023)">page needed</span>\]\]</i>\]</sup>-385][^cite_note-386][^cite_note-387][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPedraza2007\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidQCSJ61F4j34CpgPA5_5\]-388] Prior to 13 January 2013, Cuban citizens could not travel abroad, leave or return to Cuba without first obtaining official permission along with applying for a government-issued passport and travel visa, which was often denied.[^cite_note-389] Those who left the country typically did so by sea, in small boats and fragile rafts.

On 9 September 1994, the U.S. and Cuban governments agreed that the U.S. would grant at least 20,000 visas annually in exchange for Cuba's pledge to prevent further unlawful departures on boats.[^cite_note-390]

In the 2020s Cuba is undergoing its most severe socioeconomic crisis since the [fall of the Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dissolution_of_the_Soviet_Union), leading to a record number of Cubans fleeing the island.[^cite_note-\:12-391] The number of Cubans trying to enter the United States, primarily through the [Mexican border](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Borders_of_Mexico), surged from 39,000 in 2021 to over 224,000 in 2022. In 2022, more than 2% of the population (almost 250,000 Cubans out of 11 million) migrated to the United States, and thousands more went to other countries, a number "larger than the 1980 [Mariel boatlift](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mariel_boatlift) and the [1994 Cuban rafter crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1994_Cuban_rafter_crisis) combined", which were Cuba's previous largest migration events.[^cite_note-Augustin-11-12-22-305] Many resorted to selling their homes at very low prices to afford one-way flights to [Nicaragua](21362.md), hoping to travel through Mexico to reach the U.S.[^cite_note-\:12-391] Internal migration has led to overpopulation in Havana, resulting in people living in makeshift shelters or overcrowded buildings, some of which are on the brink of collapse. Regular power outages harken back to the early 1990s, a time when Soviet subsidies ended, plunging the island into economic hardship.[^cite_note-\:12-391] Emigration has continued into the 2020s, with the national population dipping below 10 million for the first time since 1980 in 2025. This signifies a 13% loss of population since 2012, when the Cuban population peaked at 11.2 million.[^cite_note-392]

Cuba's "Special Period" saw the country relying heavily on foreign tourism and the earnings of nationals working abroad. The 2020 pandemic, however, severely affected this revenue stream, decreasing the number of tourists by 75% in 2020. Monetary reforms in 2021 introduced shocks of inflation, further exacerbating the country's food scarcity and boosting the black market's prominence.[^cite_note-\:12-391] Despite the increasing hardships, the Cuban spirit remains resilient. Access to the internet since 2018 and widespread use of social media have fueled calls for political and economic liberalization. The power of the internet was evident during the Cuban protests of 2021, which were promptly suppressed by the police, with many prominent artists and bloggers detained.[^cite_note-\:12-391]

As of 2013 the top emigration destinations were the United States, Spain, Italy, Puerto Rico, and Mexico.[^cite_note-393]

### Languages

Main article: [Cuban Spanish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_Spanish)



A trilingual sign encouraging social distancing in Cuba during the Covid-19 pandemic, written in Spanish, English, and Russian

The official language is Spanish, and the vast majority of Cubans speak [Cuban Spanish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_Spanish), a form of [Caribbean Spanish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caribbean_Spanish). [Lucumí](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lucum%C3%AD_language), a dialect of the West African language [Yoruba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yoruba_language), is also used as a [liturgical language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liturgical_language) by practitioners of [Santería](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santer%C3%ADa),[^cite_note-394] and so only as a second language.[^cite_note-395] [Haitian Creole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haitian_Creole) is the second-most spoken language and is spoken by [Haitian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haitians) immigrants and their descendants.[^cite_note-396] Other languages spoken by immigrants include [Galician](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galician_language) and [Corsican](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corsican_language).[^cite_note-397] Russian will be the first foreign language taught in schools, as of the 2026-2027 school year.[^cite_note-398]

### Religion

Main article: [Religion in Cuba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_Cuba)



[Havana Cathedral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Havana_Cathedral), built between 1748 and 1777

Cuba is officially a secular state. Religious freedom increased through the 1980s,[^cite_note-399] with the government amending the constitution in 1992 to drop the state's characterization as atheistic.[^cite_note-Domínguez_2003_4-400] In 2010, the [Pew Forum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pew_Forum) estimated that religious affiliation is 59.2% Christian, 23% unaffiliated, 17.4% [folk religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Folk_religion) (such as [Santería](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santer%C3%ADa)), and the remaining 0.4% consisting of other religions.[^cite_note-pewrel-401] In a 2015 survey sponsored by Univision, 44% of Cubans said they were not religious, and 9% did not give an answer while only 34% said they were Christian.[^cite_note-402]

[Roman Catholicism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Catholicism) is the largest religion, with its origins in Spanish colonization. Despite less than half of the population identifying as Catholics in 2006, it nonetheless remains the dominant faith.[^cite_note-catholic-403] Pope John Paul II and Pope Benedict XVI visited Cuba in 1998 and 2011, respectively, and Pope Francis visited in 2015.[^cite_note-404][^cite_note-405] Prior to each papal visit, the government pardoned prisoners as a humanitarian gesture.[^cite_note-406][^cite_note-407]

The government's relaxation of restrictions on [house churches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_church) in the 1990s led to an explosion of [Pentecostalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pentecostalism), with some groups claiming as many as 100,000 members. However, [Evangelical Protestant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evangelical_Protestant) denominations, organized into the umbrella Cuban Council of Churches, remain much more vibrant and powerful.[^cite_note-Edmonds-408] Several well-known Cuban religious figures have operated outside the island, including the  humanitarian and author [Jorge Armando Pérez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jorge_Armando_P%C3%A9rez).

The religious landscape of is strongly defined by [syncretisms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syncretisms) of various kinds. Christianity is often practiced in tandem with Santería, a mixture of Catholicism and mostly African faiths, which include a number of cults. La Virgen de la Caridad del Cobre (the Virgin of *Cobre*) is the Catholic patroness of Cuba, and a symbol of Cuban culture. In Santería, she has been syncretized with the goddess [Oshun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oshun). A breakdown of the followers of Afro-Cuban religions showed that most practitioners of [Palo Mayombe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palo_Mayombe) were black and dark brown-skinned, most practitioners of [Vodú](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_Vod%C3%BA) were medium brown and light brown-skinned, and most practitioners of Santería were light brown and white-skinned.[^cite_note-409]

Cuba hosts small communities of [Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_in_Cuba) (500 in 2012), [Muslims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_Cuba) (6,000 in 2011), and members of the [Baháʼí Faith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bah%C3%A1%CA%BC%C3%AD_Faith).[^cite_note-410]

### Education

Main article: [Education in Cuba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_Cuba)



[University of Havana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Havana), founded in 1728

The [University of Havana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Havana) was founded in 1728, and there are several other well-established [colleges and universities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_universities_in_Cuba). In 1957, just before Castro came to power, the literacy rate was as low as fourth in Latin America at 76% according to the United Nations, yet higher than in Spain.[^cite_note-asce-120] Castro created an entirely state-operated system and banned private institutions. School attendance is compulsory from ages six to the end of basic secondary education (normally at age 15), and all students, regardless of age or gender, wear school uniforms with the color denoting grade level. Primary education lasts for six years, secondary education is divided into basic and pre-university education.[^cite_note-siteresources.worldbank.org-411] Cuba's [literacy rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Literacy_rate) of 99.8 percent[^cite_note-factbook-312][^cite_note-Mdgs.un.org-412] is eight points higher than the Caribbean average[^cite_note-\:15-31] and the [tenth-highest globally](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_literacy_rate), largely due to the provision of free education at every level.[^cite_note-LiteracyC-413] The high school graduation rate is 94 percent.[^cite_note-414]

Higher education is provided by universities, higher institutes, higher [pedagogical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pedagogical) institutes, and higher [polytechnic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_institutions_using_the_term_%22institute_of_technology%22_or_%22polytechnic%22) institutes. The Ministry of Higher Education operates a distance education program that provides regular afternoon and evening courses in rural areas for agricultural workers. Education has a strong political and ideological emphasis, and students progressing to higher education are expected to have a commitment to the goals of Cuba.[^cite_note-siteresources.worldbank.org-411] Cuba has provided free education to foreign nationals from disadvantaged backgrounds at the [Latin American School of Medicine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_American_School_of_Medicine_\(Cuba\)).[^cite_note-415][^cite_note-416]

### Health

Main article: [Healthcare in Cuba](4726127.md)



Life expectancy development in Cuba

After the revolution, Cuba established a free public health system.[^cite_note-\:1-34] Cuba's [life expectancy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Life_expectancy) at birth is 80.1 years (77.8 for males and 82.6 for females), ranked 58th in the world and 5th in the Americas.[^cite_note-417] Infant mortality declined from 32 infant deaths per 1,000 live births in 1957, to 10 in 1990–95,[^cite_note-418] 6.1 in 2000–2005 and 5.13 in 2009.[^cite_note-Mdgs.un.org-412][^cite_note-factbook-312] Historically, Cuba has ranked high in numbers of medical personnel and has made significant contributions to world health since the 19th century.[^cite_note-asce-120]

Cuba has [universal health care](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_health_care), and despite persistent shortages of medical supplies, there is no shortage of medical personnel.[^cite_note-whiteford-419] Primary care is available throughout the island, and infant and maternal mortality rates compare favorably with those in developed nations.[^cite_note-whiteford-419] That an impoverished nation like Cuba has health outcomes rivaling the developed world is referred to by researchers as the Cuban Health Paradox.[^cite_note-420] Cuba ranks 29th on the 2024 Bloomberg Healthiest Country Index, the highest ranking of a developing country.[^cite_note-421] The healthcare system, renowned for its medical services, has emphasized the export of health professionals through international missions, aiding global health efforts.[^cite_note-\:9-422] However, while these missions generate significant revenue and serve as a tool for political influence, domestically, Cuba faces challenges including medication shortages and disparities between medical services for locals and foreigners.[^cite_note-\:9-422] Despite the income from these missions, only a small fraction of the national budget has been allocated to public health, underscoring contrasting priorities within the nation's healthcare strategy.[^cite_note-\:9-422]

Disease and infant mortality increased in the 1960s immediately after the revolution, when half of Cuba's 6,000 doctors left the country.[^cite_note-423] Recovery occurred by the 1980s,[^cite_note-bethell-107] and the country's health care has been widely praised.[^cite_note-Feinsilver_1989_4to5-424] The Communist government stated that universal health care was a priority of state planning and progress was made in rural areas.[^cite_note-425] After the revolution, the government increased rural hospitals from one to 62.[^cite_note-\:1-34] Like the rest of the Cuban economy, medical care suffered from severe material shortages following the end of Soviet subsidies in 1991, and a tightening of the U.S. embargo in 1992.[^cite_note-426] Challenges include low salaries for doctors,[^cite_note-\:7-35] poor facilities, poor provision of equipment, and the frequent absence of essential drugs.[^cite_note-\:8-36]

Cuba has the highest doctor-to-population ratio in the world and has sent thousands of doctors to more than 40 countries around the world.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBreierWildschut200716,_81-427] According to the [World Health Organization](33583.md), Cuba is "known the world over for its ability to train excellent doctors and nurses who can then go out to help other countries in need".[^cite_note-who140914-428] In 2014 there were around 50,000 Cuban-trained health care workers aiding 66 nations.[^cite_note-429] Cuban physicians have played a leading role in combating the [Ebola virus epidemic in West Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ebola_virus_epidemic_in_West_Africa).[^cite_note-430] [Preventative medicine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Preventative_medicine) is very important within the Cuban medical system, which provides citizens with easy to obtain regular health checks.[^cite_note-\:1-34]

Import and export of [pharmaceutical drugs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pharmaceutical_drug) is done by the Quimefa Pharmaceutical Business Group (FARMACUBA) under the Ministry of Basic Industry. This group also provides technical information for the production of these drugs.[^cite_note-431] Isolated from the West by the US embargo, Cuba developed the successful lung cancer vaccine, [Cimavax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CimaVax-EGF), which is now available to US researchers for the first time, along with other novel Cuban cancer treatments. The vaccine has been available for free to the Cuban population since 2011.[^cite_note-432] According to [Roswell Park Comprehensive Cancer Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roswell_Park_Comprehensive_Cancer_Center) CEO Candace Johnson: "They've had to do more with less, so they've had to be even more innovative with how they approach things. For over 40 years, they have had a preeminent immunology community."[^cite_note-433] During the thaw in Cuba–U.S. relations, many U.S. lung cancer patients traveled to Cuba to receive vaccine treatment. The end of the thaw under the Trump Administration resulted in a tightening of travel restrictions, making it harder for U.S. citizens to travel to Cuba for treatment.[^cite_note-434]

In 2015, Cuba became the first country to eradicate [mother-to-child transmission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mother-to-child_transmission) of HIV and syphilis,[^cite_note-435] a milestone hailed by the [World Health Organization](33583.md) as "one of the greatest public health achievements possible".[^cite_note-436]

## Culture



A local musical house, Casa de la Trova in Santiago de Cuba

[Cuban culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Cuba) is influenced by its melting pot of cultures, primarily those of [Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Spain), West Africa, and the indigenous [Guanahatabey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guanahatabey) and Taíno. After the 1959 revolution, the government started a national literacy campaign, offered free education to all and established rigorous [sports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sport_in_Cuba), ballet, and music programs.[^cite_note-hsas-437]

### Architecture



The 18th-century entrance of the [Castillo del Príncipe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Castillo_del_Pr%C3%ADncipe) in Havana, photo taken in 1997

[Cuban architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architecture_of_Cuba) reflects a range of historical influences, with its foundations rooted primarily in the Spanish colonial period. Early settlements, or villas, were typically organized around a central church, surrounded by residential structures. These homes often featured interior courtyards and iron grilles, a characteristic element of colonial domestic architecture. Spanish [Baroque architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baroque_architecture) played a significant role during this era, exemplified by religious buildings such as the [Basílica de San Francisco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basilica_of_San_Francisco_de_As%C3%ADs,_Havana) in Havana. In response to the threat of piracy, large fortifications were also constructed, contributing to the island's defensive infrastructure.

Several historic urban centers developed during the colonial period, many of which have been recognized for their architectural and cultural value. Notable examples include Havana, [Camagüey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camag%C3%BCey), [Cienfuegos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cienfuegos) and [Trinidad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trinidad,_Cuba), all of which are inscribed as UNESCO World Heritage Sites. These cities display a variety of architectural styles, ranging from Baroque and [Neoclassical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neoclassical_architecture) to [eclectic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eclecticism) influences. Additional towns such as [Santiago de Cuba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santiago_de_Cuba), [Matanzas](1386915.md) or [Remedios](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Remedios,_Cuba) also preserve significant colonial-era architecture.

During the Republican period (1902–1959), Cuban architecture saw the construction of prominent public and commercial buildings. Notable examples include [El Capitolio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Capitol_of_Cuba), inspired by the [United States Capitol](31979.md), as well as modern high-rise structures such as the [FOCSA Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FOCSA_Building) and the [Habana Hilton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tryp_Habana_Libre) (later renamed Habana Libre). Architect [Antonio Quintana Simonetti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antonio_Quintana_Simonetti) emerged as a leading figure during the mid-20th century, contributing to the development of modern Cuban architecture.

Following revolution, architectural styles were heavily influenced by Soviet urban planning, emphasizing functionality, uniformity, and efficiency. Residential neighborhoods constructed during this period reflected the characteristics of socialist realism seen in cities like [Moscow](19004.md) and [Minsk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minsk). After the dissolution of the Soviet Union and the end of the Cold War, architecture became more varied. The 1990s and early 21st century saw a rise in the construction of luxury hotels, incorporating modern materials such as glass and steel, and reflecting global architectural trends similar to those found in cities like [Mexico City](18987.md), [Caracas](55289.md), and [New York](645042.md).

### Literature

[Cuban literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_literature) began to find its voice in the early 19th century. Dominant themes of independence and freedom were exemplified by José Martí, who led the Modernist movement in Cuban literature. Writers such as [Nicolás Guillén](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicol%C3%A1s_Guill%C3%A9n) and José Z. Tallet focused on literature as social protest. The poetry and novels of [Dulce María Loynaz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dulce_Mar%C3%ADa_Loynaz) and [José Lezama Lima](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Lezama_Lima) have been influential. Romanticist [Miguel Barnet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miguel_Barnet), who wrote *Everyone Dreamed of Cuba*, reflects a more melancholy Cuba.[^cite_note-438]

[Alejo Carpentier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alejo_Carpentier) was important in the [magic realism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magic_realism) movement. Writers such as [Reinaldo Arenas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reinaldo_Arenas), [Guillermo Cabrera Infante](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guillermo_Cabrera_Infante), and [Daína Chaviano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Da%C3%ADna_Chaviano), [Pedro Juan Gutiérrez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pedro_Juan_Guti%C3%A9rrez), [Zoé Valdés](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zo%C3%A9_Vald%C3%A9s), [Guillermo Rosales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guillermo_Rosales) and [Leonardo Padura](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leonardo_Padura) have earned international recognition in the post-revolutionary era, though many of these have felt compelled to continue their work in exile due to ideological control of media by the Cuban authorities. However, some Cuban writers continue living and writing in Cuba, including Nancy Morejón.[^cite_note-439]

### Music





[Gloria Estefan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gloria_Estefan) and [Celia Cruz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Celia_Cruz)

[Cuban music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Cuba) is rich and is the most commonly known expression of Cuban culture. The central form of this music is *[son](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Son_cubano)*, which has been the basis of many other musical styles like "[Danzón](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Danz%C3%B3n) de nuevo ritmo", [mambo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mambo_\(music\)), [cha-cha-chá](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cha-cha-ch%C3%A1_\(music\)) and [salsa music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salsa_music). Rumba ("de cajón o de solar") music originated in the early Afro-Cuban culture, mixed with Spanish elements of style.[^cite_note-440] The [tres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tres_\(instrument\)) was invented in Cuba from Spanish cordophone instruments models (the instrument is actually a fusion of elements from the Spanish guitar and lute). Other traditional Cuban instruments are of African origin, Taíno origin, or both, such as the [maracas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maraca), [güiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/G%C3%BCiro), [marímbula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mar%C3%ADmbula) and various wooden drums including the [mayohuacán](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mayohuac%C3%A1n).

Popular Cuban music of all styles has been enjoyed and praised widely across the world. Cuban classical music, which includes music with strong African and European influences, and features symphonic works as well as music for soloists, has received international acclaim thanks to composers like [Ernesto Lecuona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ernesto_Lecuona). Havana was the heart of the [rap](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rap) scene in Cuba when it began in the 1990s.&#x20;

### Dance

Main article: [Dance from Cuba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dance_from_Cuba)

The culture encompasses a wide range of dance forms.[^cite_note-441] Danzón was the official musical genre and dance of Cuba.[^cite_note-Urfé_1965-442] Mambo music and dance developed originally in Cuba, with further significant developments by Cuban musicians in Mexico and the US. The [cha-cha-cha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cha-cha-cha_\(dance\)) is another dance of Cuban origin,[^cite_note-443] while the Cuban [bolero](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bolero) originated in Santiago de Cuba in the 19th century.[^cite_note-444] [Concert dance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Concert_dance) is supported by the government and includes internationally renowned companies such as the [Ballet Nacional de Cuba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ballet_Nacional_de_Cuba).[^cite_note-Johnn-445] [Salsa dancing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salsa_\(dance\)) originated in Cuba, and [Cuban salsa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_salsa) is danced around the world.

### Media

Main article: [Mass media in Cuba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass_media_in_Cuba)



Users of a public WiFi hotspot in Havana, Cuba

[ETECSA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ETECSA) opened 118 cybercafes across the country in 2013.[^cite_note-bedroses-446] The government of Cuba provides an online encyclopedia website called [EcuRed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EcuRed) that operates in a "[wiki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wiki)" format.[^cite_note-reuters-447] Internet access is controlled, and e-mail is closely monitored.[^cite_note-rsf-448]

Since 2018, access to Internet by mobile data is available. In 2019, 7.1 million Cubans could access the Internet.[^cite_note-449] The prices of connections, since WiFi zones, or mobile data, or from houses through "Nauta Hogar" service have been decreasing, especially since the economic reform of January 2021, when all the salaries increased by at least 5 times, and the prices of Internet remain in the same point.[^cite_note-450][^cite_note-451] In 2024, it was reported that 8.19 million Cuban people have Internet access.[^cite_note-\:2-452] There were 6.68 million mobile connections in Cuba in January 2021.[^cite_note-\:2-452]

### Cuisine



A traditional meal of *ropa vieja* (shredded flank steak in a tomato sauce base), black beans, yellow rice, plantains and fried yuca with beer

[Cuban cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_cuisine) is a fusion of [Spanish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_cuisine) and [Caribbean cuisines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caribbean_cuisine). Cuban recipes share spices and techniques with Spanish cooking, with some Caribbean influence in spice and flavor. Food rationing, which has been the norm for the last four decades, restricts the common availability of these dishes.[^cite_note-453] The traditional meal is not served in courses; all food items are served at the same time.

The typical meal could consist of plantains, black beans and rice, *[ropa vieja](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ropa_vieja)* (shredded beef), [Cuban bread](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_bread), pork with onions, and tropical fruits. Black beans and rice, referred to as *[moros y cristianos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moros_y_Cristianos_\(food\))* (or *moros* for short), and plantains are staples. Many of the meat dishes are cooked slowly with light sauces. Garlic, cumin, oregano, and bay leaves are the dominant spices.

### Sports

Main article: [Sport in Cuba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sport_in_Cuba)

Due to historical associations with the United States, many Cubans participate in sports that are popular in North America, rather than sports traditionally played in other Latin American nations. [Baseball](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baseball_in_Cuba) is the most popular. Other popular sports include volleyball, [boxing](4243.md), [athletics](43719.md), [wrestling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wrestling), basketball and [water sports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_sports).[^cite_note-454] Cuba is a dominant force in [amateur boxing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amateur_boxing), consistently achieving high medal tallies in major international competitions. Boxers [Rances Barthelemy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rances_Barthelemy) and [Erislandy Lara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Erislandy_Lara) defected to the U.S. and Mexico respectively.[^cite_note-455][^cite_note-456] Cuba also provides a [national team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuba_at_the_Olympics) that competes in the [Olympic Games](22576.md).[^cite_note-457] [Jose R. Capablanca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jose_R._Capablanca) was a Cuban [world chess champion from 1921 to 1927](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Chess_Championship#Capablanca,_Alekhine_and_Euwe_\(1921%E2%80%931946\)).

[^cite_note-63]: &#x20;After the French captured Havana in 1555, the governor's son, Francisco de Angulo, went to the [Viceroyalty of New Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Spain).<sup>[\[58\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWright1916246-62)</sup>

[^cite_note-76]: &#x20;According to one military historian, "The thirty-eight individuals who responded to the call for independence on 9 October 1868 had almost no military experience. They, and other Cubans, ignorant of the martial skills, were soon joined by a small band of political refugees from Santo Domingo. A number of these individuals had fought for Spain in Santo Domingo following its re-annexation (1861–65). When Spain quit Santo Domingo for the second time, some Dominican colonial officers immigrated to Cuba. Most were unable to find service in the Spanish army in Cuba. Some of these former soldiers joined the new Revolutionary army and provided its initial training and leadership."<sup>[\[69\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEScheina2003353-74)</sup> Mercenaries from Canada, Colombia, France, Mexico, and the United States joined the Cuban Revolutionary Army. Chinese nationals, brought to Cuba as indentured servants, also fought for the cause.<sup>[\[69\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEScheina2003353-74)</sup> By 1876, the Cuban Revolutionary movement was facing internal strife, largely driven by racial tensions. General Máximo Gómez surrendered his command after officers refused to follow his orders because he was Dominican. At the same time, the campaign against Antonio Maceo, a [mulatto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mulatto) leader, grew stronger as white factions sought to undermine his leadership because of his race. These racial divisions contributed to a decline in morale within the Revolutionary Army.<sup>[\[70\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEScheina2003357-75)</sup>

[^cite_note-134]: &#x20;At least 18 U.S. servicemen were killed by those mines.<sup>[\[25\]](#cite_note-hrw-29)</sup>

With a single line in his journal, Columbus described a group of Taino natives smoking rolled tobacco as they rested in the woods near Cuba's Bahia Bariay harbor that November: "The Spaniards upon their journey met with great multitudes of people, men and women with firebrands in their hands and herbs to smoke after their custom." Columbus, convinced about great riches in the New World, pursued only dreams of gold. The explorer did not realize that Europe would soon fall under the spell of tobacco and the rest of the world would follow suit.

Through an elaborate process of consultation with and suggestions from mass organizations, municipal assemblies, and local work centers, the Candidacy Commissions (now chaired by trade union, rather than PCC, representatives) produced slates of nominees with just one candidate per seat. Voters only had the choice of voting yes or no.

Cuba's scientific achievements, while limited, are also being shared with other Third World countries, thereby furthering Cuban influence and prestige abroad."

[^cite_note-Gleijeses_1996_159,161-234]: &#x20;[Gleijeses 1996](#CITEREFGleijeses1996), pp. 159, 161: "Cuba's relationship with Algeria in 1961–5 ... clashes with the image of Cuban foreign policy—cynical ploys of a \[Soviet] client state—that prevails not only in the United States but also in many European capitals. ... The aid Cuba gave Algeria in 1961–2 had nothing to do with the East-West conflict. Its roots predate Castro's victory in 1959 and lie in the Cubans' widespread identification with the struggle of the Algerian people."

[^cite_note-Gleijeses_2010_327-235]: &#x20;[Gleijeses 2010](#CITEREFGleijeses2010), p. 327: "The dispatch of 36,000 Cuban soldiers to Angola between November 1975 and April 1976 stunned the world; ... by 1988, there were 55,000 Cuban soldiers in Angola."

[^cite_note-Gleijeses_2002_392-236]: &#x20;[Gleijeses 2002](#CITEREFGleijeses2002), p. [392](https://books.google.com/books?id=QHWGwG71hzMC\&pg=PA392): "After Angola, Cuba's largest military intervention was in Ethiopia, where in 1978 16,000 Cuban troops helped repulse the invading Somali army."

[^cite_note-Gleijeses_1997_50-238]: &#x20;[Gleijeses 1997](#CITEREFGleijeses1997), p. 50: "On 14–16 October 1960, \[Guinean president Ahmed Sékou] Touré went to Havana. It was the first visit of an African chief of state to Cuba. The following year Cuba's foreign aid programme to Third World governments began when fifteen students from Guinea arrived in Havana to attend the university or technical institutes."

[^cite_note-Gleijeses_1997_45-239]: &#x20;[Gleijeses 1997](#CITEREFGleijeses1997), p. 45: "Joining the rebellion in 1966, and remaining through the war's end in 1974, this was the longest Cuban intervention in Africa before the despatch of troops to Angola in November 1975.
